An explicit algorithm for the Higman Embedding Theorem
V. H. Mikaelian

Abstract
. We propose an algorithm which for any recursive group G G , given by its effectively enumerable generators and recursively enumerable relations, outputs an explicit embedding of G G into a finitely presented group directly written by its generators and defining relations. This is the explicit analogue of the remarkable Higman Embedding Theorem stating that a finitely generated group G G is embeddable into a finitely presented group if and only if G G is recursive. The constructed finitely presented group can even be chosen to be 2 2 -generator. This algorithm has already been applied, for example, to the additive group of rational numbers Q {\mdmathbb Q} , which is recursive. The question on explicit embedding of Q {\mdmathbb Q} into a finitely presented group was mentioned in the literature by Johnson, De la Harpe, Bridson and others. The suggested method can be used to solve the problem of embeddings for some other recursive groups, also. The embedding algorithm is built using conventional free constructions and their modifications, including free products with amalgamation, HNN-extensions, and certain auxiliary ✻ -constructions. We also analyze the steps of original Higman embedding to indicate which of its parts are or are not explicit.



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
20F05, 20E06, 20E07.

“We do fundamental research, not only to acquire

results solely, but because the process is an ennobling one”

Graham Higman, 1987

1.
Introduction

Our objective is to suggest an explicit analogue of the Higman Embedding Theorem proven in the fundamental article
[
15
]
, i.e., an algorithm that for a given recursive group
G
constructively outputs its explicit embedding into a finitely presented group
\mathcal{G}
given by its generators and defining relations directly, see the definitions and references in Section
2.1
.
Algorithm
1.1
is outlined in Section
1.2
below, and it is based on some auxiliary facts and constructions from our recent work
[
30
,
31
,
33
]
.

Higman shows
possibility
of such an embedding by proving that a group with finitely many generators (or a group with effectively enumerable countably infinite generators) is embeddable into a finitely presented group, as soon as it is recursive, see Theorem 1 and Corollary on p. 456 in
[
15
]
.
Since it is a simple combinatorial observation that inside a finitely presented group any subgroup generated by a set of effectively enumerated generators is recursive, the main part of arguments in
[
15
]
is to prove sufficiency in Theorem 1, i.e.,
existence
of embedding; and that is the part we plan to make explicit.

1.1.
On explicitness of the embedding in
[
15
]

Higman has never alluded the embedding of
[
15
]
is explicit.
Moreover, for some groups with very uncomplicated recursive presentations it is (or was until recently) an open problem to explicitly embed them into finitely presented groups, see sections
1.3
,
1.4
below.
However, in
[
42
]
Valiev remarks:

“G. Higman’s proof is constructive in the sense that from constructive description of the group
G
one can, in principle, extract a description for [the finitely presented group], but this construction is so vast that it is practically impossible to do this.”

Since this remark of Valiev has become a subject for discussions, we found it appropriate to first cover the point about explicitness of
[
15
]
before construction of its explicit analogue.

We outline the main steps of the Higman construction in Section
3.1
, and then state in Section
3.2
which parts are
not
explicit in it, see points
3.2.1
–
3.2.3
and
Conclusion
in
3.2.4
.

1.2.
The explicit embedding algorithm

The detailed description of the explicit embedding method
with examples and references to actual proofs in the later sections, is given in Chapter
4
.
The embedding process can be outlined via the following pseudocode:

Algorithm 1.1

(Explicit embedding of a recursive group into a finitely presented group)
.

We are given a recursive group
G=\langle\,X\mathrel{|}R\,\rangle
defined on an effectively enumerable alphabet
X=\{a_{1},a_{2},\ldots\}
by a set of recursively enumerable relations
R
.

Output an explicit embedding of
G
into a finitely presented group
\mathcal{G}
given by its generators and defining relations explicitly.

1.

Mapping each generator
a_{i}
to the “universal word”
a_{i}(x,y)
from (
4.1
)
in Section
4.1
,
write for each relation
w\in R
a new relation
w^{\prime}(x,y)
in
F_{2}=\langle x,y\rangle
, and denote by
R^{\prime}
the set of all such new
w^{\prime}(x,y)
.
Set
T_{\!G}=F_{2}/\langle\,R^{\prime}\,\rangle^{\!F_{2}}
to be the
2
-generator group explicitly given via
\langle\,x,y\mathrel{|}R^{\prime}\,\rangle
.

2.

Construct the injective embedding
\alpha:G\to T_{G}
by mapping each
a_{i}
to its image
a_{i}(x,y)
in
T_{\!G}
, see Theorem
4.1
.
The group
T_{\!G}
is recursive by
Corollary
4.2
.

3.

For each relation (
4.7
) on two variables
x,y
in
R^{\prime}
, output the sequence of integers
(
4.8
),
see Section
4.2
. Denote
\mathcal{X}
to be the set of such sequences “coding” the set of words
R^{\prime}
.

4.

Construct
\mathcal{X}

from
{\mathcal{Z}}
and
{\mathcal{S}}
via the Higman operations (
H
) in one of
two
ways. Either by the
long
process following Higman’s original method involving the functions
f(n,r)
,
a(r)
,
b(r)
in
[
15
]
, see sections
3.2
and
4.3
below. Or, alternatively, when
\mathcal{X}
satisfies certain conditions, use the much
shorter
process from
[
31
]
, see Section
4.3
below.

5.

The subgroup
A_{\!\mathcal{X}}
corresponding to
\mathcal{X}
, see Section
2.4
, is benign in
F=\langle a,b,c\rangle
, see Section
2.5
: there is a finitely presented overgroup
K_{\!\mathcal{X}}
of
F
and a finitely generated subgroup
L_{\!\mathcal{X}}\leq K_{\!\mathcal{X}}
such that
F\cap L_{\!\mathcal{X}}=A_{\!\mathcal{X}}
.
Using the proof steps for Theorem
A
in Chapter
6
, explicitly output
K_{\!\mathcal{X}}
by its generators and defining relations, and indicate
L_{\!\mathcal{X}}
by its generators. Namely, write the groups

K_{\!\mathcal{X}}
,
L_{\!\mathcal{X}}
for two sets

\mathcal{X}={\mathcal{Z}},{\mathcal{S}}
, see Section
6.2
, and then for each of operations (
H
) (used to construct
\mathcal{X}
from from
{\mathcal{Z}}
and
{\mathcal{S}}
in Step
4
) apply sections
6.3
,
6.5
–
6.11
outputting a new pair
K_{\!\mathcal{X}}
,
L_{\!\mathcal{X}}
after each operation.

6.

Load the final groups
K_{\!\mathcal{X}}
,
L_{\!\mathcal{X}}
outputted in previous Step
5
into Theorem
B
to build the explicit embedding
\beta:T_{\!\mathcal{X}}\to\mathcal{G}
of the group
T_{\!\mathcal{X}}
from
(
7.1
) into a finitely presented group
\mathcal{G}
, see sections
7.2
–
7.6
including the “The Higman Rope Trick” and the explicit presentation of
\mathcal{G}
in (
7.19
).

7.

Since our process was via the
2
-generator group
T_{\!G}
, the above
T_{\!\mathcal{X}}
from
Step
6
coincides with
T_{\!G}
from Step
1
.
I.e., as an explicit embedding
\varphi
of the initial group
G
into the finitely presented group
\mathcal{G}
we can output the composition
\varphi:G\to\mathcal{G}
of
\alpha:G\to T_{G}
from (
4.3
) with
\beta:T_{\!\mathcal{X}}\to\mathcal{G}
from (
7.15
), see Section
7.7
.

8.

[
Optional
]

G
can be embedded into a
2
-generator finitely presented group
T_{\!\mathcal{G}}
by the composition
\psi
of
\varphi:G\to\mathcal{G}
from Step
7
with the embedding
\gamma:\mathcal{G}\to T_{\!\mathcal{G}}
in Section
7.9
.

1.3.
An application of the algorithm for
{\mdmathbb Q}

As non-trivial applications of Algorithm
1.1
we in
[
33
]
suggested explicit embeddings of the additive group of rational numbers
{\mdmathbb Q}
into a finitely presented group
\mathcal{Q}
, and into a
2
-generator finitely presented group
T_{\!\mathcal{Q}}
.

Being countable, the group
{\mdmathbb Q}
certainly has an embedding into a finitely generated group by the
remarkable theorem of Higman, Neumann and Neumann
[
14
]
. Since
{\mdmathbb Q}
apparently has a recursive presentation, such as (
4.5
), the above finitely generated group can even be finitely presented by
[
15
]
.
The question of whether this (natural) embedding can be
explicit
, is addressed on p. 53 in the monograph of De la Harpe
[
13
]
,
in Problem 14.10 (a) of Kourovka Notebook
[
21
]
by Bridson and De la Harpe,  in Johnson’s work
[
19
]
, etc. Moreover, discussing the Higman Embedding
Theorem in
[
19
]
Johnson for certain recursive groups builds their explicit embeddings into finitely presented ones. Then he expresses his gratitude to Higman for raising that problem, and mentions:
“Our main aim, of embedding in a finitely presented group the additive group of rational numbers continues to elude us”
, see p. 416 in
[
19
]
.

The steps of Algorithm
1.1
have been applied for the group
{\mdmathbb Q}
in
[
33
]
: a finitely presented group
\mathcal{Q}
, and a finitely presented
2
-generator group
T_{\!\mathcal{Q}}
with the embeddings

\varphi:{\mdmathbb Q}\to\mathcal{Q}

and

\psi:{\mdmathbb Q}\to T_{\!\mathcal{Q}}

are explicitly given in sections 9.1 and 9.2 in
[
33
]
.
Both of these embeddings were reported earlier in
[
29
]
without any proofs yet.

The first explicit examples with proofs for finitely presented groups holding
{\mdmathbb Q}
, were presented by
Belk, Hyde and Matucci in
[
4
]
.
Their first embedding of
{\mdmathbb Q}
in
[
4
]
is into the group
\overline{T}
from
[
11
]
, namely,
\overline{T}
is the group of all piecewise-linear homeomorphisms
f:\mdmathbb R\to\mdmathbb R
satisfying certain specific requirements, see Theorem 1 in
[
4
]
.
The second finitely presented group is the automorphism group of Thompson’s group
F
, see Theorem 2 in
[
4
]
. These embeddings allow further variations, say,
\overline{T}
(together with its subgroup
{\mdmathbb Q}
) admits an embedding into two specific finitely presented simple groups
T\!\mathcal{A}
and
V\!\mathcal{A}
.

See also the discussion in
[
43
]
where some additional mentions to this problem are attested, including personal recollections about how this question has been mentioned at the past conferences by Martin Bridson, Pierre de la Harpe, Laurent Bartholdi.

This topic also concerns the
Boone-Higman conjecture
proposing that a finitely generated group has solvable word problem if and only if it can be embedded into a finitely presented simple group. The survey
[
5
]
puts into context known results on this conjecture and the construction in
[
4
]
.

Also, Section 3 in
[
5
]
outlines some motivation for development of interesting parallelism between group theory and mathematical logic, leading to the Higman Embedding Theorem and to Boone-Higman conjecture.

1.4.
The problem of explicit embedding for
GL(n,{\mdmathbb Q})
, other possible problems

Problem 14.10 in
[
21
]
contains one more question which in the current edition of Kourovka is coined as Problem 14.10 (c):

“Find an explicit and “natural” finitely presented group
\Gamma_{n}
and an embedding
of
GL(n,{\mdmathbb Q})
in
\Gamma_{n}
.
Another phrasing of the same problems is: find a simplicial complex
X
which
covers a finite complex such that the fundamental group of
X
is
{\mdmathbb Q}
or, respectively,

GL(n,{\mdmathbb Q})
.”

This question was recently mentioned in
[
5
]
also.

We would like to announce that, as another application of Algorithm
1.1
,
an explicit embedding of
GL(n,{\mdmathbb Q})
into some finitely presented
\Gamma_{n}
reflecting this question is suggested. Moreover, that group
\Gamma_{n}
can even be
2
-generator
[
35
]
.

If the reader is aware of
any other open problems
on explicit embeddings of recursive groups into finitely presented groups, it would be great to mention them in the discussion
[
45
]
. Needless to say, all mentions will be thankfully reflected in any publication in which we use information kindly shared in
[
45
]
.

1.5.
Other embedding methods

After Higman’s paper
[
15
]
a few further articles also appeared to prove the same theorem by other methods, see

[
41
,
1
,
2
]
, etc. The textbooks

[
23
,
39
]
reflect and modify their techniques, see also the Appendix in
[
40
]
.

The reason why we for Algorithm
1.1
stick with the methods of Higman is not only the fact that
[
15
]
is the oldest and most celebrated approach, but also that it is based on the
classic
definition of recursion via composition, primitive recursion and minimization applied to the zero, successor and projections functions, see
[
10
,
38
,
7
]
.
The above cited other methods, on the contrary, are deducing recursion to “third party” results, such as,
the solution of Hilbert’s Tenth Problem
[
24
]
showing that a subset of
{\mdmathbb Z}^{n}
is recursively enumerable if and only if it is Diophantine;
the interpretation of Turing machines via semigroups
[
1
,
39
]
;
the Boone-Britton group
[
8
,
9
]
; the group diagrams
[
39
]
, etc.

1.6.
Auxiliary notation and references

In Chapter
2
we for future use collect some notation, definitions and references related to recursion, Higman operations, benign subgroups, free constructions.

The proofs below are very dependent on constructions that we have proposed over recent years in
[
30
,
31
,
32
,
33
]
.
Since we want to avoid any repetition of fragments from older articles, we collect some brief results without any proofs in sections

4.1
,

5.1
,

5.2
,

5.3
.

In particular, in Section
4.1
we present the algorithm for explicit embedding of a countable group
G
into a
2
-generator group
T_{\!G}
from
[
30
]
. We use it as the first tool for our embedding, see steps
1
,
2
in Algorithm
1.1
.

Section
5.1
introduces the generic
✻
-
construction
: a “nested” combination of HNN-extensions and free products with amalgamated subgroups. We suggested this construction in

[
32
]
as it simplifies and unifies many of our proofs needed for steps
5
–
7
in Algorithm
1.1
.
Sections
5.2

and

5.3
define a specific group
\mathscr{A}
and its basic properties to use them in later sections.
The group
\mathscr{A}
has already been used in
[
33
]
.

and

initial

new

and

with these features. This graphical visualization hopefully makes identification of benign subgroups inside the general constructions clearer.

-construction and some other constructions we use. Only one of those eight figures from

in Chapter

.

2.
Preliminary notation, constructions and references

2.1.
Recursive enumeration and recursive groups

Higman uses the Kleene characterization of recursively enumerable subsets of the set of non-negative integers
\mdmathbb N_{0}
, as subsets that can be obtained as images of partial recursive functions on
\mdmathbb N_{0}
, i.e., of functions that can be built from the
zero
,
successor
and
projections
functions by means of
composition

\circ
,
primitive recursion

\rho
and
minimization

\mu
operations, see
[
10
,
38
,
7
]
for background information.
A newer popular term for recursive is
computable
, but we stay closer to Higman’s traditional notation here.

Since the Gödel numbering can be used “to code” the elements of an
arbitrary
effectively enumerable set
{\mathcal{E}}
by means of integers from
\mdmathbb N_{0}\!=\!\{0,1,2,\ldots\}
, the subsets of such a set
{\mathcal{E}}
can also be “coded” by subsets of
\mdmathbb N_{0}
, and hence, the notion of recursive enumeration is generalized for such subsets of
{\mathcal{E}}
also.
In particular, if a group
G=\langle\,X\mathrel{|}R\,\rangle
is given by some effectively enumerable generators
X
, then in the free group
F=\langle\,X\rangle
on the alphabet
X
the set of
all
words apparently is effectively enumerable, and if the set of relations
R
is recursively enumerable inside it in the above sense, then
G
is called a
recursive
group.
There are other definitions of recursive groups – the condition about recursively enumerable set
R
could well be replaced by the condition that
R
is a
recursive
set (i.e.,
R
and
F\backslash R
are
both
recursively enumerable), see p. 88 in
[
23
]
. The words in
R
can even be chosen to be
positive
only, see p. 451 in
[
39
]
.

To make it clear what we mean under
“explicit”
in Algorithm
1.1
, let us agree to say that a group is given
explicitly
, if its generators and defining relations are listed (effectively enumerated) explicitly; this has especially simple meaning if they are just finitely many.
Moreover, in the proofs below such groups are going to be constructed from some explicitly known subgroups via certain free constructions.
Under an
explicit
embedding
\varphi:G\to H
we understand an embedding for which the images of all generators of
G
under
\varphi
are written as some explicit words via the generators of
H
. The explicit embedding and constructive embedding are used as synonyms here. Although the term “constructive” is used in the literature often, we do not want to limit ourselves exclusively to that term just to avoid uncomfortable wording of sort “constructive construction”.

2.2.
Integer-valued functions
f

Following
[
15
]
denote by
\mathcal{E}
the set of all functions
f:{\mdmathbb Z}\to{\mdmathbb Z}
with finite supports, such as, the function
f
sending the integers
-1,0,1,2
to
3,2,9,8
,  and all other integers to
0
.
When
f
for a certain fixed
m=1,2,\ldots
has the property that

f(i)=0
for
all

i<0
and
i\geq m
, then it is comfortable to record the function
f
as a sequence
f=(j_{0},\ldots,j_{m-1})
assuming
f(i)=j_{i}
for
i=0,\ldots,m-1
, e.g., the function
f=(2,5,3)
sending the integers
0,1,2
to
2,5,3
,  and all other integers to
0
.
Clearly,
m
may not be uniquely defined for
f
​, and where necessary we may add extra zeros at the end of a sequence, e.g., the previous function can well be recorded as
f=(2,5,3,0,0)
(the last two zeros change nothing in the way
f
acts on
{\mdmathbb Z}
). In particular, the constant zero function can be written, say, as
f=(0)
or as
f=(0,\ldots,0)
where needed.
See more in 2.2 of
[
31
]
.

Denote
{\mathcal{E}}_{m}\!=\big\{(j_{0},\ldots,j_{m-1})\;\mathrel{|}\;j_{i}\!\in\!{\mdmathbb Z},\;i\!=\!0,\ldots,m-1\big\}
for an
m=1,2,\ldots
Clearly,
{\mathcal{E}}_{m}\subseteq{\mathcal{E}}
, and by the agreement above
{\mathcal{E}}_{m^{\prime}}\subseteq{\mathcal{E}}_{m}
,
in case
m^{\prime}\leq m
.
Also denote
{\mathcal{Z}}\!=\!\big\{(0)\big\}
to consist of the constant
zero
function only, and denote

{\mathcal{S}}\!=\!\big\{(n,\,n\!+\!1)\mathrel{|}n\in{\mdmathbb Z}\big\}
to consist of all
consecutive
integer pairs from
{\mathcal{E}}_{2}
only.

For any
f\in\mathcal{E}
and
k\in{\mdmathbb Z}
define the function
f_{k}^{+}
​ as follows:

f_{k}^{+}(i)=f(i)
for all
i\!\neq\!k
, and

f_{k}^{+}(k)=f(k)\!+\!1
.
When
f\in{\mathcal{E}}_{m}
, we shorten
f_{m-1}^{+}=f^{+}
​. Say, for the above
f\!=\!(2,5,3)\in{\mathcal{E}}_{3}
we have
f_{1}^{+}\!\!=(2,6,3)
and

f^{+}\!\!=(2,5,4)
, we just add
1
to the last coordinate of
f
to get
f^{+}
​.

2.3.
The Higman operations

Higman defines the following specific basic operations that transform the subsets of
{\mathcal{E}}
to some new subsets of
{\mathcal{E}}
, see Section 2 in
[
15
]
:

(H)

\iota,\;\upsilon,\;\rho,\;\sigma,\;\tau,\;\theta,\;\zeta,\;\pi,\;\omega_{m},

m=1,2,\ldots

Although we are going to use the Higman operations extensively, we do not include their definitions and main properties here, referring the reader to Section 2 in
[
15
]
or to Section 2.3 in
[
31
]
.

Also, in Section 2.4 in
[
31
]
we suggested some extra auxiliary Higman operations which hopefully make the work with the subsets of
{\mathcal{E}}
more natural and intuitive:

(H*)

\sigma^{i},\;\zeta_{i},\;\zeta_{S},\;\pi^{\prime},\;\pi_{i},\;\pi^{\prime}_{i},\;\tau_{k,l},\;\alpha,\;\epsilon_{S},\;+,\;\iota_{n},\;\upsilon_{n}.

If a subset
\mathcal{X}
of
{\mathcal{E}}
can be obtained by operations (
H
) and (
H*
), then it can be obtained by operations (
H
) alone.

2.4.
Defining subgroups by integer sequences

Let
F=\langle a,b,c\rangle
be a free group of rank
3
. For any
i\in{\mdmathbb Z}
denote
b_{i}=b^{c^{i}}=c^{-i}b\,c^{i}
in
\langle b,c\rangle
. Then for any function
f\!\in{\mathcal{E}}
one can define in
F
the following products in
\langle b,c\rangle
and in
F
:

(2.1)

b_{f}=\cdots b_{-1}^{f(-1)}b_{0}^{f(0)}b_{1}^{f(1)}\cdots\quad\text{and}\quad a_{f}=a^{b_{f}}=b^{-1}_{f}a\,b_{f}.

Say, for the function

f
sending
-1,0,1,2
to
3,2,9,8
in Section
2.2
we have

b_{f}\!=\!b_{-1}^{3}b_{0}^{2}b_{1}^{9}b_{2}^{8}
.

When
f
is in
{\mathcal{E}}_{m}
, we can more comfortably record it as

f=(j_{0},\ldots,j_{m-1})
, see Section
2.2
, and then write

b_{f}=b_{0}^{j_{0}}\cdots b_{m-1}^{j_{m-1}}
.
Say, for
f\!=\!(2,5,3)

we may put

b_{f}\!=\!b_{(2,5,3)}\!=b_{0}^{2}b_{1}^{5}b_{2}^{3}
and

a_{(2,5,3)}\!=a^{b_{0}^{2}b_{1}^{5}b_{2}^{3}}
.

For a set
\mathcal{X}
of sequences from
{\mathcal{E}}
denote by
A_{\mathcal{X}}
the subgroup generated in
F
by all the conjugates
a_{f}=a^{b_{f}}
with
f\!\in\mathcal{X}
.
Say,
A_{{\mathcal{E}}_{2}}
is the subgroup generated in
F
by all words of type

a^{b_{(j_{0},\;j_{1})}}=b_{1}^{-j_{1}}b_{0}^{-j_{0}}\cdot a\cdot b_{0}^{j_{0}}b_{1}^{j_{1}}
, with all
j_{0},j_{1}\in{\mdmathbb Z}
.

For technical purposes we may use the above notation with some
other
free generators, also. Say, in the free group
\langle d,e\rangle

we may set

d_{i}=d^{e^{i}}
​,

d_{f}=\cdots d_{-1}^{f(-1)}d_{0}^{f(0)}d_{1}^{f(1)}\cdots

Or in the free group
\langle g,h,k\rangle

we may set

h_{i}=h^{k^{i}}
​,

h_{f}=\cdots h_{-1}^{f(-1)}h_{0}^{f(0)}h_{1}^{f(1)}\cdots

and

g_{f}=g^{h_{f}}
​.
Or else, we may take the isomorphic copy

\bar{F}=\langle\bar{a},\bar{b},\bar{c}\rangle
of
F=\langle a,b,c\rangle
, and use inside it the elements
\bar{b}_{i},\bar{b}_{f},\bar{a}_{f}
and the subgroup
\bar{A}_{\mathcal{X}}
defined as expected.

2.5.
Benign subgroups

A subgroup
H
in a finitely generated group
G
is called a
benign subgroup
in
G
, if
G
can be embedded into a finitely presented group
K
with a finitely generated subgroup
L\leq K
such that
G\cap L=H
.
For detailed information on benign subgroups we refer to Sections 3, 4 in
[
15
]
, see also sections 2, 4, 5 in
[
32
]
.

Remark 2.1
.

From the definition of benign subgroup it is very easy to see that arbitrary
finitely generated
subgroup
H
in any
finitely presented
group
G
is benign in
G
. Because
G
itself can be chosen as the finitely presented overgroup
K_{H}
of
G
with a finitely generated subgroup
H=L_{H}
, such that

H\cap L_{H}=H\cap\,H=H
inside
K_{H}=G
. We are going to often use this remark in the sequel.

2.6.
Free constructions

For background information on free products with amalgamations and on HNN-extensions we refer to

[
6
]
and
[
23
]
.
Our usage of the
normal forms
in
free constructions is closer to
[
6
]
.
Notation varies in the literature, and to maintain uniformity we are going to adopt notation we used in
[
32
]
.

Namely, if any groups
G
and
H
have subgroups, respectively,
A
and
B
isomorphic under
\varphi:A\to B
, then the (generalized) free product of
G
and
H
with amalgamated subgroups
A
and
B
is denoted by

G*_{\varphi}H
(we are
not
going to use the alternative notation
G*_{A=B}H
). When
G
and
H
are overgroups of the same subgroup
A
, and
\varphi
is just the identical isomorphism on
A
, we write the above as
G*_{A}H
.

If
G
has subgroups
A
and
B
isomorphic under
\varphi:A\to B
, then the HNN-extension of the base
G

by some stable letter
t

with respect to the isomorphism

\varphi
is denoted by

G*_{\varphi}t
.
In case when
A=B
and
\varphi
is identity map on
A
, we denote
G*_{A}t
.
We also use HNN-extensions
G*_{\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},\ldots}(t_{1},t_{2},\ldots)

with more than one stable letters, see
[
32
]
for details.

Below we are going to use a series of facts about certain specific subgroups in free constructions
G*_{\varphi}H
,
G*_{A}H
,
G*_{\varphi}t
and
G*_{A}t
. We have collected them in Section 3 of
[
32
]
to refer to that section whenever needed.

Notice that some of the constructions in this article could be replaced by shorter analogs using the wreath product methods we suggested in

[
25
]
–
[
28
]
. However, here we intentionally use free constructions only to stay closer to the methods of Higman.

2.7.
The “conjugates collecting” process

We are going to use the following simple, well known combinatorial trick.
If
{\mathfrak{X}}
and
{\mathfrak{Y}}

are any disjoint subsets in a group
G
, then it is easy to verify that any element
w\in\langle{\mathfrak{X}},{\mathfrak{Y}}\rangle
can be written as:

(2.2)

w=u\cdot v=x_{1}^{\pm v_{1}}x_{2}^{\pm v_{2}}\cdots x_{k}^{\pm v_{k}}\cdot v

where
v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{k},\,v\in\langle{\mathfrak{Y}}\rangle
, and

x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k}\in{\mathfrak{X}}
.
Indeed, first write
w
as a product of some elements from
{\mathfrak{X}}
, from
{\mathfrak{Y}}
and of their inverses.
Next, by grouping
the elements from
{\mathfrak{Y}}
where necessary, and by adding some auxiliary trivial elements we rewrite
w
as:

(2.3)

w=z_{1}^{\vphantom{8}}x_{1}^{\pm 1}z_{2}^{\vphantom{8}}\,x_{2}^{\pm 1}z_{3}^{\vphantom{8}}\cdots z_{k}^{\vphantom{8}}\,x_{k}^{\pm 1}z_{k+1}^{\vphantom{8}}

where
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\in{\mathfrak{X}}
and

z_{1},\ldots,z_{k+1}\in\langle{\mathfrak{Y}}\rangle
.
Say, if
{\mathfrak{X}}=\{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\}
and

{\mathfrak{Y}}=\{y_{1},y_{2}\}
, then

w=x_{2}^{-1}y_{1}^{3}y_{2}^{\vphantom{8}}x_{1}^{2}x_{3}^{\vphantom{8}}
can be rewritten as

w=z_{1}\,x_{2}^{-1}z_{2}^{\vphantom{8}}x_{1}z_{3}^{\vphantom{8}}x_{1}z_{4}^{\vphantom{8}}x_{3}^{\vphantom{8}}z_{5}^{\vphantom{8}}
,
where

z_{1}=1
,

z_{2}=y_{1}^{3}y_{2}^{\vphantom{8}}
,

z_{3}=z_{4}=z_{5}=1
are in
\langle{\mathfrak{Y}}\rangle
. Then (
2.3
) can be transformed to:

w=z_{1}^{\vphantom{8}}x_{1}^{\pm 1}z_{1}^{-1}\!\cdot\,z_{1}^{\vphantom{8}}z_{2}^{\vphantom{8}}\,x_{2}^{\pm 1}(z_{1}^{\vphantom{8}}z_{2}^{\vphantom{8}})^{-1}\!\cdot\,z_{1}^{\vphantom{8}}z_{2}^{\vphantom{8}}z_{3}^{\vphantom{8}}\cdots(z_{1}^{\vphantom{8}}\!\cdots z_{k}^{\vphantom{8}})\,x_{k}^{\pm 1}(z_{1}^{\vphantom{8}}\!\cdots z_{k}^{\vphantom{8}})^{-1}z_{1}^{\vphantom{8}}\!\cdots z_{k}^{\vphantom{8}}z_{k+1}^{\vphantom{8}},

which is (
2.2
) for

v_{1}^{\vphantom{8}}\!=z_{1}^{-1}
​​​,

v_{2}^{\vphantom{8}}\!=(z_{1}z_{2})^{-1}
​​​​,

v_{3}^{\vphantom{8}}\!=(z_{1}z_{2}z_{3})^{-1}
​​​​,
\ldots

v_{k}^{\vphantom{8}}\!=(z_{1}\cdots z_{k})^{-1}
​​​​,

v\!=z_{1}^{\vphantom{8}}\!\cdots\;z_{k}^{\vphantom{8}}z_{k+1}^{\vphantom{8}}
.

In a simplest case
{\mathfrak{X}}=\{x\}
and
{\mathfrak{Y}}=\{y\}
are of carnality
1
, and in the
2
-generator group
G=\langle x,y\rangle
we can write any
w\in G
as a product
of some conjugates of
x
with a power of
y
:

(2.4)

w=x^{\pm y^{n_{1}}}\!x^{\pm y^{n_{2}}}\!\!\cdots\,x^{\pm y^{n_{s}}}\!\!\cdot y^{k}=\!u\cdot v\,.

As we will see later, this argument is helpful in HNN-extensions and other free constructions.

3.
Higman’s original construction

3.1.
The main steps of construction in
[
15
]

Let us start by a summary of the main steps of Higman’s construction in sections 2–5 of
[
15
]
by which he embeds a recursive group
G
into a finitely presented group.
We outline the construction in below five steps because it is presented that way at the end of
“Introduction”
in
[
15
]
.

These five steps will be used for two purposes below:

–

in Section
3.2
we use them to state which are the
non-explicit
parts of
[
15
]
;

–

and in Chapter
4
we use them to explain
what we modify
to
[
15
]
to overcome the found issues, and to have an explicit embedding.

3.1.1.
Step 1.

Characterization of the recursively enumerable subsets of
{\mathcal{E}}
via Higman operations, page 457 and Section 2 in
[
15
]

Higman begins by the classic Kleene characterization of a recursively enumerable subsets of
\mdmathbb N_{0}
which we mentioned in Section
2.1
with references.
The Gödel numbering allows to generalize the notion of recursively enumerable subsets of
\mdmathbb N_{0}
to
any
subsets of an
effectively enumerable
set
{\mathcal{E}}
, in particular, of the set
{\mathcal{E}}
of all integer-valued functions
f\!:{\mdmathbb Z}\to{\mdmathbb Z}
with finite supports from the above Section
2.2
, as this
{\mathcal{E}}
certainly is effectively enumerable.

The first main technical result of
[
15
]
is Theorem 3 that suggests an alternative characterization for recursively enumerable subsets
\mathcal{X}
of the above
{\mathcal{E}}
, without using the Kleene characterization and the Gödel numbering. Namely, they are the subsets constructed from two specific basic sets

{\mathcal{Z}}
and

{\mathcal{S}}
, see Section
2.2
above, by means of the Higman operations

\iota,\;\upsilon,\;\rho,\;\sigma,\;\tau,\;\theta,\;\zeta,\;\pi,\;\omega_{m}
,
see (
H
) in Section
2.3
.
The set of all such subsets of
{\mathcal{E}}
is denoted via
\mathscr{S}
​.

3.1.2.
Step 2.

Characterization of the recursively enumerable subsets of
{\mathcal{E}}
via benign subgroups in the free group of rank
3
, sections 3 and 4 in
[
15
]

In the above Step 1 Higman uses practically no group-theoretical constructions yet; they actually occur in the current step.
In the free group
F\!=\!\langle a,b,c\rangle
, using the conjugates
b_{i}=b^{c^{i}}
​,  the specific elements
b_{f}
and
a_{f}
are being defined for any function
f\!\in{\mathcal{E}}
, see (
2.1
) in
Section
2.4
above.
This allows to define the respective subgroup
A_{\!\mathcal{X}}=\langle a_{f}\;|\;f\in\mathcal{X}\rangle
inside
F
for any subset
\mathcal{X}
of
{\mathcal{E}}
.

Theorem 4 in
[
15
]
states that
\mathcal{X}
is recursively enumerable in
{\mathcal{E}}
if and only if the respective subgroup
A_{\!\mathcal{X}}
is
benign
in
F
, see definition and references for benign subgroups in Section
2.5
above.
Thus, discussion of recursive enumeration for subsets of
{\mathcal{E}}
is being translated to the language of benign subgroups.

These Step 1 and Step 2 occupy the main part of
[
15
]
(sections 2 – 4), while the remaining three steps fit into a couple of pages in Section 5
“Conclusion”
of Higman’s work.

3.1.3.
Step 3.

Characterization of the recursively enumerable subsets in free groups of rank
2
, p. 473, Section 5 in
[
15
]

Lemma 5.1 in
[
15
]
establishes connection between
all
recursively enumerable subsets in the free group of rank
2
and benign subgroups of a certain
specific type
in the free group of rank
3
.

3.1.4.
Step 4.

Characterization of the recursively enumerable subgroups in free groups, p. 474, Section 5 in
[
15
]

As an adaptation of previous step, Lemma 5.2 in
[
15
]
shows that in
any
finitely generated free group, a subgroup is recursively enumerable if and only if it is benign.
It is enough to argument this claim for the free group of rank
2
.

3.1.5.
Step 5.

The final embedding by “Higman Rope Trick”, pp. 474 – 475, Section 5 in
[
15
]

Let the given finitely generated recursive group
G
have a presentation
G=\langle\,X\mathrel{|}R\,\rangle
with a finite
X
and a recursively enumerable
R
. Then
G=F/\langle R\rangle^{F}
holds for a free group
F
of rank
|\,X|
. Since
R
is recursively enumerable, its normal closure
\langle R\rangle^{F}
​ also is recursively enumerable for very simple combinatorial reasons and, hence, by previous step it is benign in
F
.

Thus, by Lemma 3.5 in
[
15
]
, providing alternative definitions for benign subgroups, the free product
H
of two copies of
F
amalgamated in
\langle R\rangle^{F}
can be embedded into a certain finitely presented group
K
.

Finally, a specific HNN-extension of the direct product
K\!\times\!G
is being constructed to be the finitely presented overgroup of
G
we are looking for. A process often called the “Higman Rope Trick”, see the discussion
[
44
]
, shows that all but
finitely
many of the relations of this HNN-extension are redundant.

3.2.
Is Higman’s embedding constructive?

Suspicion, that not all steps of Higman’s original construction
[
15
]
may be constructive, arise already from the fact that for some well-known recursive groups the problem of their explicit embedding into finitely presented groups has been (or still is) open for decades.
See, for example, the question on explicit embedding of the recursive group
{\mdmathbb Q}
into a finitely presented group asked in
[
13
,
21
,
19
]
and outlined in Section
1.3
above, including the cited remark of Johnson about embedding of
{\mdmathbb Q}
that
“continues to elude us”
.
Yet another example of this type is the matrix group
GL(n,{\mdmathbb Q})
mentioned in Section
1.4
.

As far as we know, neither Higman himself nor his descendants had ever stated explicitness of the embedding of
[
15
]
in the literature.

Besides such indirect arguments of “historical” nature, it is reasonable to analyze the steps of Higman embedding
[
15
]
to directly indicate its parts which suggest
no explicit algorithm
to accomplish them, i.e., to state what makes some parts of
[
15
]
non-constructive.

With this objective in mind, suppose a recursive group is explicitly given as
G=\langle\,X\mathrel{|}R\,\rangle
, and try to follow Higman’s construction
literally
to see which obstacles we face.

Remark 3.1
.

Notice that in our modified constructions in the proofs of
Theorem
A

and
Theorem
B

below we are
not
going to use those steps causing obstacles. We mention those parts just to study explicitness of
[
15
]
.

3.2.1.
Construction of
\mathcal{X}
and
A_{\!\mathcal{X}}
from
R

To build the finitely presented group
K
used in Step 5 for the given recursive group
G=\langle\,X\mathrel{|}R\,\rangle
, see
3.1.5
above,
[
15
]
starts from the recursively enumerable relations set
R
in the free group
F
of rank
|\,X|
. As the enumeration of
R
is very easy to continue on its normal closure,
\langle R\rangle^{F}
​ also is recursively enumerable (
[
15
]
denotes this closure by
R
).

Then “moving backwards” via Higman’s
Step 4 – Step 2 for
\langle R\rangle^{F}
​, one should arrive to the respective subset
\mathcal{X}
inside
{\mathcal{E}}
, and to the benign subgroup
A_{\!\mathcal{X}}
inside the free group
\langle a,b,c\rangle
of rank
3
.

To do this Higman first embeds the group
F
into a free group
F_{2}=\langle x,y\rangle
of rank
2
, see p. 474 in
[
15
]
, using any of the well known textbook methods, check the chapters concerning the subgroups of free groups in any of

[
37
,
39
,
20
,
6
]
.
By the remark preceding Lemma 3.8 in
[
15
]
, the normal subgroup
\langle R\rangle^{F}
is benign in
F
if and only if it is benign in
F_{2}
.
Hence, we can imagine all the relations from
\langle R\rangle^{F}
are rewritten in just
two
letters
x,y
.
The purpose of this passage is that it allows to trivially output the integer sequences
f\in\mathcal{X}
from words on
x,y
. For example, from the relation
x^{2}y^{5}x^{3}\in F_{2}
one can extract the sequence
f\!=\!(2,5,3)\in\mathcal{X}
. This
f
later is being used in the free group
\langle a,b,c\rangle
to produce the product

b_{f}=b_{0}^{2}b_{1}^{5}b_{2}^{3}
and the conjugate
a_{f}\!=a^{b_{f}}
inside
A_{\mathcal{X}}
,
see Section
2.4
above.

In the next steps of
[
15
]
Higman is going to use this benign subgroup
A_{\mathcal{X}}
of
\langle a,b,c\rangle

to build, for the benign subgroup
\langle R\rangle^{F}
of
F_{2}
, the respective finitely presented overgroup
K_{\mathcal{X}}
with a finitely generated subgroup
L_{\mathcal{X}}\leq K_{\mathcal{X}}
, such that
F_{2}\cap L_{\mathcal{X}}=\langle R\rangle^{F}
holds.

This passage, regretfully, creates two hurdles for the coming steps in
[
15
]
:

Firstly
, Higman’s process outputs the groups
K_{\!\mathcal{X}}
and
L_{\!\mathcal{X}}

not
directly for the initial
F
from the original presentation
G=F/\langle R\rangle^{F}
​,  but for the auxiliary free group
F_{2}
. Thus, following
[
15
]
, we have to “return back” from
F_{2}
to
F
in order to modify the obtained
K_{\!\mathcal{X}}
and
L_{\!\mathcal{X}}
for the free group
F
of rank
|\,X|
.
That part is not hard to do by a few routine steps because by the remark before Lemma 3.8 in
[
15
]
, a group is benign in
F_{2}
if and only if it is benign in
F
.
In order
not
to introduce new notation, suppose the above
K_{\mathcal{X}}
and
L_{\mathcal{X}}
are those found for
F
, that is,
F\cap L_{\mathcal{X}}=\langle R\rangle^{F}
holds inside a certain finitely presented
K_{\mathcal{X}}
for the recursive subgroup
\langle R\rangle^{F}
​.

Secondly
, the passage from
R
to
\langle R\rangle^{F}
very much amplifies the set of relations we have to work with. For, even if the group
G=\langle\,X\mathrel{|}R\,\rangle
is defined with some plainly simple set
R
, the subgroup
\langle R\rangle
and the normal closure
\langle R\rangle^{F}
may contain a by far
wider
stockpile of relations, which generates a by far
wider
set
\mathcal{X}
, and makes it even harder to write that set via the operations
(
H
)
in the coming steps.
The reader could take, say, the
3
-generator direct power
G={\mdmathbb Z}^{3}
​ of the cycle
{\mdmathbb Z}
, and starting from its
three
defining relations, create an infinite set of new relations in the closure
\langle R\rangle^{F}
. Then each of these new relations provides a new sequence
f
to be included in the “exploding” set
\mathcal{X}
, which then has to be built via (
H
).

Remark 3.2
.

These two obstacles could already be sufficient for us to refrain from naming the construction in
[
15
]
an explicit one. Yes, some additional proofs can be added to it to overcome these barriers at the cost of complicating the whole process.
In any case, our embedding in Algorithm
1.1
does
not
have to use those steps, at all, see Remark
3.1
, because using the intermediate group
T_{G}
we explicitly have the much smaller version of set
\mathcal{X}
at our disposal by very different means.
Also in
[
33
]
we did not have to overcome those two hurdles to build the explicit embedding for
{\mdmathbb Q}
, as we obtained the respective set
\mathcal{X}
via a certain auxiliary group
T_{{\mdmathbb Q}}
with with a much more manageable set of relations.

Anyway, in order to be able to proceed to Higman’s next step, assume the set
\mathcal{X}
is given somehow, be it some description via Kleene characterization, or equivalently, via a Turing machine, or via another comparable format.

3.2.2.
Explicit construction of
\mathcal{X}
via the Higman operations

According to Step 2, see
3.1.2
above,
A_{\!\mathcal{X}}
is benign if and only if the set
\mathcal{X}
is recursively enumerable in
{\mathcal{E}}
; and by Step 1, see
3.1.1
, that only happens when the set of functions
\mathcal{X}
belongs to
\mathscr{S}
​. Hence, the next objective is to
explicitly
construct
\mathcal{X}
via the Higman operations
(
H
) from
{\mathcal{Z}}
and
{\mathcal{S}}
.

Lemma 2.8 in
[
15
]
provides a long but
yet explicit
algorithm to construct certain partial recursive functions
f(n,r)
,
a(r)
,
b(r)
, with
r=0,1,2,\ldots
, which one-by-one “record” all the sequences
g\in\mathcal{X}
, and “mark” their start- and end points, in the sense that for any such
g
there is some
r
for which
g(n)=f(n,r)
for all
n\in{\mdmathbb Z}
, while
f(n,r)=0
for all the remaining coordinates
n<a(r)
and
n>b(r)
.
Such functions

f_{{\mathcal{E}}}(n,r)
,

a_{{\mathcal{E}}}(r)
,

b_{{\mathcal{E}}}(r)

are explicitly built first for the case of the whole set
\mathcal{X}={\mathcal{E}}
, and then for any other generic recursively enumerable subset
\mathcal{X}\subseteq{\mathcal{E}}
there exists a partial recursive function
h(s)
such that the
r
’th function
f_{{\mathcal{E}}}(n,r)
corresponds to a function in
\mathcal{X}
if and only if
r=h(s)
for some
s
. Existence of such
h(s)
is clear, as
\mathcal{X}
is recursively enumerable, and it consists of functions with finite supports only.

Then for every multi-variable integer-based function
f(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
its
graph
is being defined before Lemma 2.2, and
\mathscr{F}
is denoted to be the set of all functions whose graphs are in
\mathscr{S}
. Lemmas 2.1 – 2.7 show that
\mathscr{F}
contains all the partial recursive functions.
Hence, the graphs of the above functions
f,a,b
also are in
\mathscr{F}
, i.e., their graphs can be constructed via the operations (
H
).

Using the above steps, Higman finishes the proof of Theorem 3 on
pp
. 463 – 464 by showing how the earlier picked sequence
g\in\mathcal{X}
can constructively be obtained via (
H
) using the graphs of these functions
f,a,b
.

Although each of the listed steps is doable, and is explicit, the
only
way to construct
\mathcal{X}
via (
H
) suggested in
[
15
]
is to present the functions
f,a,b
by Kleene’s characterization, that is, to construct
f,a,b
from the basic functions

n(x)=0
,

s(x)=x\!+\!1
,

u_{m}^{i}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m})

by means of composition
\circ
, primitive recursion
\rho
, and minimization
\mu
, and then to apply a series of operations (
H
) for each of the steps of that characterization to construct their graphs via (
H
), then to load these into the explicit proof of Theorem 3.

These proofs certainly are constructive, but they require such a vast routine of steps that their application to non-trivial groups is a hard task. In construction of our explicit embeddings for recursive groups, in particular for the group
{\mdmathbb Q}
in
[
33
]
, we never follow it, see Remark
3.1
. We use the much shorter constructions from
[
31
]
where applicable.

3.2.3.
Missing steps in construction of
K_{\!\mathcal{X}}
and
L_{\!\mathcal{X}}

Now assume the set
\mathcal{X}
has already been constructed from
{\mathcal{Z}}
and

{\mathcal{S}}
via some known sequence of operations (
H
). Try to directly follow the steps of proof for Theorem 4, see lemmas 4.4 – 4.10 in
[
15
]
, in order to explicitly construct the finitely presented overgroup
K_{\!\mathcal{X}}
and its finitely generated subgroup
L_{\!\mathcal{X}}
for the benign subgroup
A_{\!\mathcal{X}}
in
\langle a,b,c\rangle
. There are two reasons which do
not
allow us to use these proofs from
[
15
]

directly
:

Firstly
, Higman notices on p. 468 in
[
15
]
that if
A
is a subgroup of two finitely generated groups
G
and
H
which are embeddable into some finitely presented group, then
A
is benign in both
G
and
H
, or in neither. For the theoretical purposes of
[
15
]
this makes it very convenient to sometimes “switch” the group in which we show
A
is benign. This shortens the proofs of lemmas 4.4 – 4.10 very much, but this is
not
sufficient for our practical purposes, because after each Higman operation we wish to always keep the current
A_{\!\mathcal{X}}
benign in the
same
group
\langle a,b,c\rangle
, necessarily, so that we are in position to apply the next operation as many times as needed, to get the terminal group
A_{\!\mathcal{X}}
again benign in
\langle a,b,c\rangle
.

Secondly
,
[
15
]
often uses Lemma 3.7 stating that if
H
is the image of
G
under a homomorphism
\phi
, then under some conditions
A
is benign in
G
if and only if
\phi(A)
is benign in
H
. Again, this shortens the proofs in
[
15
]
very much, as it sometimes is enough to show that the given
A_{\!\mathcal{X}}
is an image (or a pre-image) of a known benign subgroup. But this is
not
sufficient for purposes of this work because one has to make sure if the function
\phi
itself is
computable
, and because extra steps must be added to go from the finitely presented overgroup of
\phi(A)
(or of
\phi^{-1}(A)
) to that of
A
.

At the end of the construction, as we warned in Point
3.2.1
, when the groups
K_{\!\mathcal{X}}
and
L_{\!\mathcal{X}}
have been constructed,
[
15
]
has to “come back” from
F_{2}
to modify them for the case of group
F
from the original representation
G=F/\langle R\rangle^{F}
of our initial group
G=\langle\,X\mathrel{|}R\,\rangle
. Our construction below does not need this step, see Remark
3.1
.

At the very end
[
15
]
proceeds to Step 5 with the “Higman Rope Trick” for some HNN-extension of the direct product
K\!\times\!G
mentioned in
3.1.5
above. The proofs of this step are constructive, we can use them with minimal adaptation.

3.2.4.
Conclusion

The above points in

3.2.1
–
3.2.3
show that
the original embedding in
[
15
]
involves steps that are either non-constructive (at least, in their current form), or steps that require extra amendments to become constructive. Even for some uncomplicated groups, such as the readily finitely presented
3
-generator direct power

{\mdmathbb Z}^{3}
​, mentioned right before Remark
3.2
, we were unable to build the needed embedding by just following Higman’s steps
literally
.

Yes, it could be possible to append some new chapters to Higman’s original proofs to try to make the explicit analog of
[
15
]
.
However, that would produce a by far longer proof cluttered with details, perhaps concealing the main purpose of Higman’s work.
The central objective of the fundamental research
in
[
15
]
is to reveal deep connections between mathematical logic and group theory. Hence, overloading it with many more details could be counterproductive to achieving this major goal, we think.

Higman’s construction
can
be called constructive only in the sense that it contains
no theoretical obstacles
which could render it unadaptable by some (serious) changes that would make it constructive.
As mentioned in
1.
Introduction
, constructiveness of
[
15
]
and the quoted remark of Valiev from
[
42
]
have been a subject of conference disputations, and we found it appropriate to consider this issue here before proposing the modified construction in the chapters below.

4.
An outline of our modified construction

Some steps of Algorithm
1.1
are substantially different from Higman’s construction, while others follow
[
15
]
adding where needed some elements to guarantee explicitness of the embedding. In this chapter we look at the steps of Algorithm
1.1
, firstly, to refer the reader to the respective proofs (inside or outside this article), and secondly, to indicate the parts with substantial changes to
[
15
]
.

4.1.
The embedding
\alpha:G\to T_{\!G}
[Steps

1
,
2

in Algorithm
1.1
]

As we will see in section
4.2
and
7.7
, serious simplification for the embedding of an initial recursive group
G=\langle\,X\mathrel{|}R\,\rangle
can be achieved, if
G
first is embedded into a specific
intermediate

2
-generator recursive group
T_{\!G}
.

Being recursive the group
G
is at most
countable
, and we can apply the Higman, Neumann and Neumann Theorem
[
14
]
stating that any countable group
G
can be embedded into a

2
-generator group.
It is not hard to deduce from the original proofs in
[
14
]
that the relations of that
2
-generator group can explicitly be given, in case the relations
R
are known. However, the process of finding those relations by the methods of
[
14
]
is not very simple, and it may require long routine for some groups.

To simplify this we in
[
30
]
have suggested a specific
2
-generator group
T_{G}
together with an algorithm explicitly defining the embedding

\alpha:G\to T_{G}
,
and automatically writing down a set of defining relations for
T_{\!G}
from the given defining relations
R
of
G
.
Let us briefly outline that algorithm with some slight modification of notation from
[
30
]
.

Write our recursive group as

G=\langle\,X\mathrel{|}R\,\rangle=\langle a_{1},a_{2},\ldots\mathrel{|}R\,\rangle

where the generators
a_{1},a_{2},\ldots
are effectively enumerated, and the relations
w\in R
are recursively enumerable, i.e., they form the image of some partial recursive function, or roughly speaking, there is an algorithm writing them down one by one (in whatever order).

In the free group

F_{2}=\langle x,y\rangle
consider some specific “universal words”:

(4.1)

a_{i}(x,y)=y^{(xy^{i})^{\,2}\,x^{\!-1}}\!\!y^{-x}\!,\quad i=1,2,\ldots

If a relation

w\!\in R

is a word of length, say,
k
on letters

a_{i_{1}},\ldots,a_{i_{k}}\!\!\in X
, then replacing inside
w=w(a_{i_{1}},\ldots,a_{i_{k}})
each letter
a_{i_{j}}
by the
i_{j}
​’th word
a_{i_{j}}(x,y)
we get a new word:

(4.2)

w^{\prime}(x,y)=w\big(a_{i_{1}}\!(x,y),\ldots,a_{i_{k}}\!(x,y)\big)

on just
two
letters
x,y
in the free group
\langle x,y\rangle
, see Example
4.3
below for
{\mdmathbb Q}
.
The set
R^{\prime}
of all such new words
w^{\prime}(x,y)
in has
\langle x,y\rangle
a normal closure
\langle\,R^{\prime}\,\rangle^{\!F_{2}}
​, the factor group by which is the
2
-generator group
T_{\!G}=F_{2}/\langle\,R^{\prime}\,\rangle^{\!F_{2}}=\langle\,x,y\mathrel{|}R^{\prime}\,\rangle
we look for.
The map
\alpha
sending each generator
a_{i}
to the word
a_{i}(x,y)\!\in F_{2}
, and then to the coset

\langle\,R^{\prime}\,\rangle^{\!F_{2}}\,a_{i}(x,y)\in T_{\!G}
can be continued to the desired embedding of
G
into
T_{\!G}
:

Theorem 4.1

(Theorem 1.1 in
[
30
]
)
.

For any countable group
G=\langle a_{1},a_{2},\ldots\mathrel{|}R\,\rangle
the above map
\alpha:a_{i}\to a_{i}(x,y)
,
i=1,2,\ldots
, defines an injective embedding:

(4.3)

\alpha:G\to T_{\!G},

of
G
into the
2
-generator group

T_{\!G}=\big\langle x,y\,\mathrel{|}\,R^{\prime}\big\rangle.

For some specific cases the formula for “universal words” can be simplified. Say, for a
torsion free
group
G
the words
a_{i}(x,y)
in (
4.1
) can be replaced by shorter words:

(4.4)

a_{i}(x,y)=y^{(xy^{i})^{\,2}x^{\!-1}}\!,\quad i=1,2,\ldots,

see Theorem 3.2 in
[
30
]
.
We stress this case here because (
4.4
) was used for the (torsion free) group
{\mdmathbb Q}
in
[
33
]
, and it is going to be used in examples below.

Regardless which of formulas (
4.1
) or (
4.4
) we use, from Theorem
4.1
it is evident that:

Corollary 4.2
.

In the above notation if the group
G
is recursive, then
T_{\!G}
also is recursive.

Example 4.3
.

Let us explain
steps

1
,
2

of Algorithm
1.1
, i.e., the transaction from
G
to
T_{\!G}
, by applying them for the rational group
{\mdmathbb Q}
.
As an initial presentation for
{\mdmathbb Q}
we may take:

(4.5)

{\mdmathbb Q}=\big\langle a_{1},a_{2},\ldots\mathrel{|}a_{k}^{k}=a_{k-1},\;\,k=2,3,\ldots\big\rangle

where
a_{k}
corresponds to the rational
{1\over k!}
with
k=2,3,\ldots
, see p. 70 in
[
18
]
and elsewhere.
To apply the algorithm of
[
30
]
first rewrite each relation
a_{k}^{k}=a_{k-1}
as

w_{k}=a_{k}^{k}\,a_{k-1}^{-1}=1
. Then, using the shorter formula (
4.4
) for the (torsion free) group
{\mdmathbb Q}
,
map each letter
a_{k}
to the word

\alpha(a_{k})=y^{(xy^{k})^{\,2}y^{\!-1}}
​​.
To each relation
w_{k}
put into correspondence the new relation

w^{\prime}_{k}(x,y)=\,(y^{k})^{(xy^{k})^{\,2}x^{\!-1}}y^{-(xy^{k-1})^{\,2}x^{-1}}\!\!,

to get the embedding
\alpha:{\mdmathbb Q}\to T_{\mdmathbb Q}

of
{\mdmathbb Q}
into the
2
-generator recursively presented group:

(4.6)

T_{\mdmathbb Q}=\big\langle x,y\;\mathrel{|}\;(y^{k})^{(xy^{k})^{\,2}x^{\!-1}}y^{-(xy^{k-1})^{\,2}x^{\!-1}}\!\!,\;\;k=2,3,\ldots\big\rangle.

Any finitely presented overgroup of
T_{\mdmathbb Q}
is an overgroup for
{\mdmathbb Q}
also, so we have the freedom to continue the work to embed the
2
-generator group
T_{\mdmathbb Q}
(apparently recursive with relations (
4.6
)) into a finitely presented overgroup.
See also Remark
4.5
below.

4.2.
Using the
2
-generator group
T_{\!G}
to get the set
\mathcal{X}

[Step

3

in Algorithm
1.1
]

As we warned in
3.2.1
,
following Higman’s construction literally, we have to construct from
R
the subset
\mathcal{X}
of
{\mathcal{E}}
, and we have some serious problems in that path. In Algorithm
1.1
all those issues vanish, as we work with the
2
-generator recursive group
T_{\!G}=\langle\,x,y\mathrel{|}R^{\prime}\,\rangle
built above.
Then each relation
w^{\prime}(x,y)\in R^{\prime}
already is a word on just two letters
x,y
:

(4.7)

w(x,y)^{\prime}=x^{j_{0}}y^{j_{1}}\cdots x^{j_{2r}}y^{j_{2r+1}}

written for some integers
j_{0},j_{1},\ldots,j_{2r},j_{2r+1}
;
the cases
j_{0}=0
, or
j_{2r+1}
are
not
ruled out (e.g., a relation may start by
y
and it may end by
x
).
In such a case we can
directly write down
the sequence for
w^{\prime}
:

(4.8)

f=(j_{0},j_{1},\ldots,j_{2r},\,j_{2r+1}),

i.e., (
4.8
) is the respective function (sequence of integers) to be included into
\mathcal{X}
for the relation
w^{\prime}(x,y)
.
The set
\mathcal{X}
produced this way for all
w^{\prime}\in R^{\prime}
clearly is recursively enumerable.

See also Section
7.7
with Remark
7.1
stressing why usage of the
2
generator group
T_{\!G}=F_{2}/\langle\,R^{\prime}\,\rangle^{\!F_{2}}=\langle\,x,y\mathrel{|}R^{\prime}\,\rangle
is useful for the
last
steps of our algorithm, also.

Let us continue Example
4.3
to see how the set
\mathcal{X}
may look for the group
T_{\mdmathbb Q}
holding
{\mdmathbb Q}
:

Example 4.4
.

For the group
{\mdmathbb Q}
written via (
4.5
) we in Example
4.3
found the group
T_{\mdmathbb Q}
written via (
4.6
).
Each of its relations
w^{\prime}_{k}(x,y)
can be rewritten as:

(4.9)

w^{\prime}_{k}(x,y)=x\,y^{-k}x^{-1}y^{-k}x^{-1}\cdot y^{k}\cdot x\,y^{k}x\;y\cdot x^{-1}y^{1-k}x^{-1}\cdot y^{-1}\cdot x\,y^{k-1}\,x\,y^{k-1}x^{-1},

k=2,3,\ldots
in order to match the format of (
4.7
).
Using the passage from (
4.7
) to (
4.8
) we trivially output the respective sequence:

(4.10)

f_{k}=\big(1,-k,-1,-k,-1,\;k,\;1,\;k,\;1,\;1,-1,\;1\!\!-\!k,-1,-1,\;1,\;k\!-\!\!1,\;1,\;k\!-\!\!1,\;-1\big),

k=2,3,\ldots
, see also Section 3.2 in
[
33
]
.
Thus, we got a subset

\mathcal{X}=\big\{f_{k}\mathrel{|}k=2,3,\ldots\big\}
of
{\mathcal{E}}

to work with in the next steps.
See also Section 9 in
[
33
]
where we explicitly give two finitely presented overgroups
\mathcal{Q}
and
T_{\!\mathcal{Q}}
both holding
{\mdmathbb Q}
.

Remark 4.5
.

Compare the ease of writing the sequences (
4.10
) from
T_{\mdmathbb Q}
with the difficulties and unclear issues listed in points
3.2.1

and
3.2.2
, caused by the original construction in
[
15
]
.
In fact, we earlier started our attempts to explicitly embed
{\mdmathbb Q}
into a finitely presented group by trying to write down the set
\mathcal{X}
for
{\mdmathbb Q}
applying the methods of
[
15
]
to (
4.5
). The futility of those attempts forced us to come to the trick with
T_{\!{{\mdmathbb Q}}}
.

4.3.
Writing
\mathcal{X}
via Higman operations  [Step

4

in Algorithm
1.1
]

After the set
\mathcal{X}
is known, one has to build this set
\mathcal{X}
from
{\mathcal{Z}}
and
{\mathcal{S}}
via the operations (
H
).
As we saw in
3.2.2
above, the method with functions
f(n,r)
,
a(r)
,
b(r)
used in
[
15
]
is explicit and, theoretically, it
can
be used in Algorithm
1.1
as it is. However, it involves very many routine steps, and we have
never
used it in our particular embeddings, including those for
{\mdmathbb Q}
.

We in
[
31
]
studied some alternative methods to construct
\mathcal{X}
from
{\mathcal{Z}}
and
{\mathcal{S}}
via the operations (
H
) based on the
structure
of its sequences, see Remark 3.8, Example 4.11 and Remark 4.12 in
[
31
]
. These alternative methods do not cover the cases of all groups, but they are usable for wide classes of groups, such as the free abelian, metabelian, soluble, nilpotent groups, the quasicyclic group
{\mdmathbb C}_{p^{\infty}}
,
divisible abelian groups, etc.
In particular, for the group
{\mdmathbb Q}
this method has been applied in
[
33
]
to construct
\mathcal{X}
via some elementary steps.

Also, in
[
31
]
we in addition to (
H
) suggested certain “auxiliary” operations (
H*
) that simplify the process even more.

4.4.
Building
K_{\!\mathcal{X}}
and
L_{\!\mathcal{X}}
for the benign subgroup
A_{\!\mathcal{X}}
in
F

[Step

5

in Algorithm
1.1
]

Assume the set
\mathcal{X}
has already been built from
{\mathcal{Z}}
and
{\mathcal{S}}
using the operations (
H
) in any of two methods mentioned in the Step
4
of Algorithm
1.1
.
Using this we next build the finitely presented overgroup
K_{\!\mathcal{X}}
of
F=\langle a,b,c\rangle
, and the finitely generated subgroup
L_{\!\mathcal{X}}\leq K_{\!\mathcal{X}}
for the
benign
subgroup
A_{\!\mathcal{X}}
in
F
, such that
F\cap L_{\!\mathcal{X}}=A_{\!\mathcal{X}}
holds.
This is done in the proof of Theorem
A
in Chapter
6
which follows the general scheme of the proof for Theorem 4 in Chapter 4 of
[
15
]
with the following two main differences:

Difference 1.
As we warned in
3.2.3
above,
[
15
]
often “switches” the groups in which
A_{\!\mathcal{X}}
is benign. Theorem
A
below always makes sure the current benign subgroup
A_{\!\mathcal{X}}
is benign in
F=\langle a,b,c\rangle

necessarily
.
This is important because we may use the operations from (
H
) for many times, so that each operation accepts an initial
A_{\!\mathcal{X}}
benign in
F
(with explicitly known
K_{\!\mathcal{X}}
and
L_{\!\mathcal{X}}
), then it produces a new set
\mathcal{Y}
from
\mathcal{X}
, and outputs a new subgroup
A_{\!\mathcal{Y}}
benign again in
F
(again with explicitly known
K_{\!\mathcal{Y}}
and
L_{\!\mathcal{Y}}
).
This allows us to rename
\mathcal{Y}
by
\mathcal{X}
after each step, and to repeat such steps as many times as needed to arrive to the desired set
\mathcal{X}
by which we in Step
4
in Algorithm
1.1
have “coded” the relations of
T_{\!G}
.

Difference 2.

Theorem 4 in
[
15
]
discusses theoretical
possibility
of construction of
K_{\!\mathcal{X}}
and
L_{\!\mathcal{X}}
without writing them down explicitly for all operations (
H
), whereas in each point of Theorem
A
we make sure that
the current

K_{\!\mathcal{X}}
is explicitly written (via generators and defining relations), and
L_{\!\mathcal{X}}
is explicitly indicated (by its generators) after each application of any of Higman operations.

The proofs for this step are in Chapter
6
, and they consume the most part of this article, see sections
6.2
,
6.3
,
6.5
–
6.11
in the proof of Theorem
A
, and the respective groups
K_{\!\mathcal{X}}
and
L_{\!\mathcal{X}}
for all operations (
H
) are explicitly recorded in

6.2
,

6.3
,

6.5.3
,

6.6.3
,

6.7.2
,

6.8.2
,

6.9.5
,

6.10.4
,

6.11.6
.

4.5.
The “Higman Rope Trick” for the
2
-generator case
[Steps

6
–
8

in Algorithm
1.1
]

For a subset
\mathcal{X}\subseteq{\mathcal{E}}
we in Chapter
7
define a new group
T_{\!\mathcal{X}}
via (
7.1
), and prove Theorem
B
on its explicit embedding
\beta:T_{\!\mathcal{X}}\to\mathcal{G}
into a finitely presented group
\mathcal{G}
, provided that the groups
K_{\!\mathcal{X}}
,
L_{\!\mathcal{X}}
are explicitly known for the benign subgroup
A_{\!\mathcal{X}}
of
F
.
Theorem
B
has been proved by some minimal adaptations of the “Higman Rope Trick” from Section 5 in
[
15
]
, and the group
\mathcal{G}
is explicitly given via (
7.19
). See some related discussion in
[
44
]
.

Then we once again use the advantage that our embedding was through the
2
-generator group
T_{\!G}
, because in such a particular case
T_{\!\mathcal{X}}
simply
is equal
to the group
T_{\!G}
from Step
1
.
Hence,
\beta
is an embedding of
T_{\!G}
into
\mathcal{G}
also, and as an explicit embedding
\varphi
of the initial
G
into
\mathcal{G}
one can take the composition
\varphi:G\to\mathcal{G}
of
\alpha
from
Step
1
with this
\beta
, see Section
7.7
.

For the
optional
Step
8

in Algorithm
1.1

we need one more constructive embedding
\gamma:\mathcal{G}\to T_{\!\mathcal{G}}
of
\mathcal{G}
into a certain
2
-generator finitely presented
T_{\!\mathcal{G}}
built in Section
7.9
, again utilizing the “universal words” from
[
30
]
.
Then the embedding
\psi:G\to T_{\!\mathcal{G}}
is the composition (
7.20
) of
\varphi
with
\gamma
.

5.
Some auxiliary constructions

5.1.
The
✻
-construction

Let us begin with the brief notation of the
✻
-
construction
and its basic properties. All the proofs with many more examples are given in
[
32
]
, and here we just state them in a format comfortable for the chapters to follow.

Let
G,M,K_{1},\ldots,K_{r}
be any groups such that the conditions

G\leq M\leq K_{1},\ldots,K_{r}
, and
K_{i}\cap\,K_{j}=M
hold for any distinct indices
i,j=1,\ldots,r
.
Picking a subgroup
L_{i}\leq K_{i}
for each
i=1,\ldots,r

we first build the HNN-extensions
K_{i}*_{L_{i}}t_{i}
of the group
K_{i}
with the base subgroup
L_{i}
and with the stable letter
t_{i}
, and next using these
r
groups we construct an auxiliary “nested” free construction:

(5.1)

\Big(\cdots\Big(\big((K_{1}*_{L_{1}}t_{1})*_{M}(K_{2}*_{L_{2}}t_{2})\big)*_{M}(K_{3}*_{L_{3}}t_{3})\Big)\cdots\Big)*_{M}(K_{r}*_{L_{r}}t_{r})

by amalgamating all these HNN-extensions in their common subgroup
M
.
For the sake of briefness let us denote the above bulky construction (
5.1
) via

(5.2)

\textstyle\textrm{\footnotesize✻}_{i=1}^{r}(K_{i},L_{i},t_{i})_{M},

and also agree to set
A_{i}=G\cap\,L_{i}
for each
i
.

Figure 1.
Construction of the group
\textrm{\footnotesize✻}_{i=1}^{r}(K_{i},L_{i},t_{i})_{M}
in (
5.2
).

The group
(
5.2
)
may coincide with certain well known constructions in some specific particular cases:

Example 5.1
.

When a certain group
G
with its subgroups
A_{1},\ldots,A_{r}
is fixed, then taking
K_{i}=M=G
and
L_{i}=A_{i}
for each
i=1,\ldots,r
, we
have the HNN-extensions
G*_{A_{i}}t_{i}
, and then

\textrm{\footnotesize✻}_{i=1}^{r}(G,A_{i},t_{i})_{G}
is the amalgamation of all such
G*_{A_{i}}t_{i}
by their subgroup
G
. That is nothing but the usual HNN-extension with multiple stable letters
t_{1},\ldots,t_{r}
:

G*_{A_{1},\ldots,\,A_{r}}\!(t_{1},\ldots,t_{r}),

compare with the more general Lemma
5.4
below.

Example 5.2
.

If we again put
K_{i}=M=G
for each
i=1,\ldots,r
, and choose
trivial
subgroups
L_{i}=A_{i}=\{1\}
, then
\textrm{\footnotesize✻}_{i=1}^{r}(G,A_{i},t_{i})_{G}
simply is the ordinary free product of
G
with the free group
\langle t_{1},\ldots,t_{r}\rangle\cong F_{r}
of rank
r
:

G*\langle t_{1}\rangle*\cdots*\langle t_{r}\rangle=G*\langle t_{1},\ldots,t_{r}\rangle=G*F_{r}.

Example 5.3
.

Consider the case when
L_{i}=K_{i}=M=G
for each
i=1,\ldots,r
. Then in
G*_{G}t_{i}
conjugation by stable letter
t_{i}
just fixes the whole
G
, which means this HNN-extension is the direct product
G\times\langle t_{i}\rangle
for
\langle t_{i}\rangle\cong{\mdmathbb Z}
. Then the
✻
-construction
\textrm{\footnotesize✻}_{i=1}^{r}(G,G,t_{i})_{G}
turns out to be the
direct
product:

G\times\langle t_{1},\ldots,t_{r}\rangle\cong G\times F_{r}.

The main reason why we introduce this construction is that many of rather complicated constructions, used in
[
15
]
and elsewhere in the literature, turn out to be
particular cases
of (
5.2
) even if they are looking very differently. Hence, we find it reasonable to define one general construction and to collect its basic properties in
[
32
]
in order to refer to them wherever needed.
The below lemmas

5.4
–
5.11
are proven in sections 3, 4 in
[
32
]
.

Lemma 5.4
.

If
G\leq M\leq K_{1},\ldots,K_{r}
are groups mentioned above, then in
{\textrm{\footnotesize✻}}_{i=1}^{r}(K_{i},L_{i},t_{i})_{M}

the following equality holds:

\langle G,t_{1},\ldots,t_{r}\rangle=G*_{A_{1},\ldots,\,A_{r}}\!(t_{1},\ldots,t_{r}).

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
A_{1},\ldots,\,A_{r}
be any subgroups in a group
G
with the intersection

I=\bigcap_{\,i=1}^{\,r}\,A_{i}
.
Then in
G*_{A_{1},\ldots,\,A_{r}}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{r})
we have:

(5.3)

\textstyle G\cap G^{t_{1}\cdots\,t_{r}}=I.

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
A_{1},\ldots,\,A_{r}
be any subgroups in a group
G
with the join

J=\big\langle\bigcup_{\,i=1}^{\,r}\,A_{i}\big\rangle
.
Then in
G*_{A_{1},\ldots,\,A_{r}}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{r})
we have:

(5.4)

\textstyle G\cap\big\langle\bigcup_{\,i=1}^{\,r}\,G^{t_{i}}\big\rangle=J.

These lemmas allow to build new benign subgroups from the existing ones:

Corollary 5.7
.

If the subgroups
A_{1},\ldots,\,A_{r}
are benign in a finitely generated group
G
, then:

1.

their intersection
I=\bigcap_{\,i=1}^{\,r}\,A_{i}
also is benign in
G
;

2.

their join
J=\big\langle\bigcup_{\,i=1}^{\,r}\,A_{i}\big\rangle
also is benign in
G
.

Moreover, if the finitely presented groups
K_{i}
with their finitely generated subgroups
L_{i}
can be
given for each
A_{i}
explicitly, then the respective finitely presented overgroups
K_{I}
and
K_{J}
with finitely generated subgroups
L_{I}
and
L_{J}

can also be given for
I
and for
J
explicitly.

Check Section 4.3 in
[
32
]
to see that as
K_{I}
and
K_{J}
one may take
\textstyle{\textrm{\footnotesize✻}}_{i=1}^{r}(K_{i},L_{i},t_{i})_{M}
which evidently is
finitely presented
for
M=G
. Also, one may choose the subgroups
L_{I}=G^{t_{1}\cdots\,t_{r}}
and
L_{J}=\big\langle\bigcup_{\,i=1}^{\,r}\,G^{t_{i}}\big\rangle
which evidently are
finitely generated
.
See Figure 6 in

[
32
]
illustrating the proof of Corollary
5.7
.

Remark 5.8
.

Corollary
5.7
stresses one of the reasons why the
✻
-construction
(
5.2
) may be helpful in composition of finitely presented groups containing the given
G
. If the subgroups
A_{1},\ldots,\,A_{r}
are
not
finitely generated, then
G*_{A_{1},\ldots,\,A_{r}}\!(t_{1},\ldots,t_{r})
may
not
be finitely presented, since the non-finitely generated subgroups
A_{i}
may add infinitely many new defining relations for this HNN-extension.
However, if all
A_{i}
are
benign
, we can embed that HNN-extension into a
finitely presented

✻
-construction
\textrm{\footnotesize✻}_{i=1}^{r}(K_{i},L_{i},t_{i})_{M}
in which we have the freedom to choose as large finitely presented groups
K_{i}
as needed, just making sure the subgroups
L_{i}
and
M
are finitely generated (then they will bring just finitely many new defining relations for (
5.2
)). This trick will be used repeatedly below, and in many cases the choice
M=G
will already be enough.

The following technical fact
proved in Section 4.4 of
[
32
]
displays some “bigger” free products inside HNN-extensions and inside
✻
-constructions (
5.2
), as soon as some “smaller” free products are known inside
G
:

Corollary 5.9
.

Let
A_{1},\ldots,\,A_{r}
be any subgroups in a group
G
such that their join
J
in
G
is isomorphic to their free product

\prod_{i=1}^{r}\,A_{i}
.
Then the join

\big\langle\bigcup_{\,i=1}^{\,r}G^{t_{i}}\big\rangle

is isomorphic to the free product
\prod_{i=1}^{r}G^{t_{i}}

in
G*_{A_{1},\ldots,\,A_{r}}\!(t_{1},\ldots,t_{r})
, and hence in
\textrm{\footnotesize✻}_{i=1}^{r}(K_{i},L_{i},t_{i})_{M}
.

5.2.
Construction of the group
\mathscr{A}

Let us use the tools above to construct the group
\mathscr{A}
to use it in the proofs in the coming sections.
In the free group
\langle b,c\rangle
of rank
2
we denoted
b_{i}=b^{c^{i}}\!\!
,  see Section
2.4
.
For a fixed integer

m
define two isomorphisms

\xi_{m}
and
\xi^{\prime}_{m}
by the rules:

\xi_{m}(b)=b_{-m+1},\;\xi^{\prime}_{m}(b)=b_{-m}
,

\xi_{m}(c)=\xi^{\prime}_{m}(c)=c^{2}

of
\langle b,c\rangle
, and using them set the HNN-extension:

(5.5)

\Xi_{m}=\langle b,c\rangle*_{\xi_{m},\xi^{\prime}_{m}}(t_{m},t^{\prime}_{m}).

See Figure 7 of

[
32
]
illustrating the construction of the group
\Xi_{m}
.
We have proved the following technical lemmas in
[
32
]
:

Lemma 5.10
.

In the above notation the following equalities hold for any
m
in
\Xi_{m}
:

(5.6)

\begin{split}\langle b,c\rangle\cap\langle b_{m},t_{m},t^{\prime}_{m}\rangle&=\langle b_{m},b_{m+1},\ldots\rangle,\\
\langle b,c\rangle\cap\langle b_{m-1},t_{m},t^{\prime}_{m}\rangle&=\langle b_{m-1},b_{m-2},\ldots\rangle.\end{split}

Lemma 5.11
.

In the above notation the following equalities hold for any
m
in
\langle a\rangle*\,\Xi_{m}
:

(5.7)

\begin{split}F\cap\langle b_{m},t_{m},t^{\prime}_{m}\rangle=\langle b_{m},b_{m+1},\ldots\rangle\;\;\;{\it and}\;\;\;F\cap\langle a,b_{m},t_{m},t^{\prime}_{m}\rangle=\langle a,b_{m},b_{m+1},\ldots\rangle,\hskip 8.53581pt\\
F\!\cap\!\langle b_{m-1},t_{m},t^{\prime}_{m}\rangle\!=\!\langle b_{m-1},b_{m-2},\ldots\rangle\;\;{\it and}\;\;\,F\!\cap\!\langle a,b_{m-1},t_{m},t^{\prime}_{m}\rangle\!=\!\langle a,b_{m-1},b_{m-2},\ldots\rangle.\end{split}

These lemmas provide us with infinitely generated benign subgroups of four types inside the free groups
\langle b,c\rangle
and
F=\langle a,b,c\rangle
. In particular,

\langle b_{1},b_{2},\ldots\rangle
is benign in
F
for the finitely presented overgroup
\langle a\rangle*\Xi_{1}
, and for its finitely generated subgroup
\langle b_{1},t_{1},t^{\prime}_{1}\rangle
.
Also the subgroup
\langle a,b_{0},b_{-1},\ldots\rangle
is benign in
F
for the same finitely presented
\langle a\rangle*\Xi_{1}
and for its finitely generated subgroup
\langle a,b_{0},t_{1},t^{\prime}_{1}\rangle
.

Figure 2.
Construction of the group
\mathscr{C}
.

Use these groups to build the

✻
-construction:

(5.8)

\mathscr{C}=\Big(\big(\langle a\rangle\!*\Xi_{1}\big)*_{\langle b_{1},t_{1},t^{\prime}_{1}\rangle}u_{1}\Big)\,*_{\langle a\rangle*\,\Xi_{1}}\Big(\big(\langle a\rangle\!*\Xi_{1}\big)*_{\langle a,b_{0},t_{1},t^{\prime}_{1}\rangle}u_{2}\Big)

which is finitely presented, and which can explicitly be given by generators and defining relations via:

(5.9)

\begin{split}\mathscr{C}&=\big\langle a,b,c,t_{1},t^{\prime}_{1},u_{1},u_{2}\mathrel{\;|\;}b^{t_{1}}=b,\;b^{t^{\prime}_{1}}=b^{c^{-1}}\!\!\!\!,\;\;c^{t_{1}}=c^{t^{\prime}_{1}}=c^{2};\\
&\hskip 105.27519pt\text{$u_{1}$ fixes $b^{c},t_{1},t^{\prime}_{1}$};\;\;\;\text{$u_{2}$ fixes $a,b,t_{1},t^{\prime}_{1}$}\big\rangle\end{split}

where “fixes” means “fixes under conjugation”, e.g.,
t_{1}^{u_{1}}=t_{1}
.

By Corollary
5.7
(
2
) the join
J
of
\langle b_{1},b_{2},\ldots\rangle
and
\langle a,b_{0},b_{-1},\ldots\rangle
is benign in
F
for the finitely presented overgroup
K_{J}=\mathscr{C}
and for its finitely generated subgroup

L_{J}=\langle F^{u_{1}},F^{u_{2}}\rangle
.

Further, in
F
the subgroups

\langle b_{1},b_{2},\ldots\rangle
and
\langle a,b_{0},b_{-1},\ldots\rangle

clearly generate their
free
product.
Hence, by Corollary
5.9
the groups
F^{u_{1}}
and
F^{u_{2}}
also generate their free product
F^{u_{1}}*\,F^{u_{2}}
in
\mathscr{C}
, as well as in its subgroup:

F*_{F\,\cap\,\langle b_{1},t_{1},t^{\prime}_{1}\rangle,\;\;\;F\,\cap\,\langle a,b_{0},t_{1},t^{\prime}_{1}\rangle}(u_{1},u_{2})\;=\;F*_{\langle b_{1},b_{2},\ldots\rangle,\;\;\langle a,b_{0},b_{-1},\ldots\rangle}(u_{1},u_{2}),

see Lemma
5.4
and Lemma
5.11
. Then
arbitrary
two isomorphisms defined on
F^{u_{1}}
and on
F^{u_{2}}
can be continued to an isomorphism on the whole subgroup
F^{u_{1}}*F^{u_{2}}
inside
\mathscr{C}
.
Choose the trivial automorphism in
F^{\,u_{1}}
and the conjugation by
b^{u_{2}}
in
F^{\,u_{2}}
​,  and denote their common continuation in
F^{u_{1}}*F^{u_{2}}
via
\omega
.
Inside
F
this
\omega
leaves the elements
b_{1},b_{2},\ldots
intact, but it sends
a,b_{0},b_{-1},\ldots
to their conjugates
a^{b}\!,\,b_{0}^{b},\,b_{-1}^{b},\ldots

Figure 3.
Construction of the group
\mathscr{A}
.

Next, denote by
\delta
the isomorphism of
F
sending
a,b,c
to
a,b^{c},c
. Now we can define one of the key technical groups of this article as the HNN-extension:

\mathscr{A}=\mathscr{C}\!*_{\omega,\delta}\!(d,e)

which is finitely presented because
\mathscr{C}
is finitely presented, while
\omega
is determined by its values on just six conjugates
a^{u_{1}},b^{u_{1}},c^{u_{1}},a^{u_{2}},b^{u_{2}},c^{u_{2}}
, and

\delta
is determined by its values on just three generators
a,b,c
.
This group can explicitly be written as:

(5.10)

\begin{split}\mathscr{A}&\!=\!\big\langle a,b,c,t_{1},t^{\prime}_{1},u_{1},u_{2},d,e\mathrel{\;|\;}b^{t_{1}}\!=\!b,\;b^{t^{\prime}_{1}}\!=\!b^{c^{-1}}\!\!\!\!,\;c^{t_{1}}\!=\!c^{t^{\prime}_{1}}\!=\!c^{2};\\[-2.0pt]
&\hskip 48.36967pt\text{$u_{1}$ fixes $b^{c}\!,t_{1},t^{\prime}_{1}$};\;\;\;\;\text{$u_{2}$ fixes $a,b,t_{1},t^{\prime}_{1}$};\\[-2.0pt]
&\hskip 48.36967pt\text{$d$ fixes
$a^{u_{1}}$\!,\,
$b^{u_{1}}$\!,\,
$c^{u_{1}}$};\\[-2.0pt]
&\hskip 48.36967pt\text{$d$ sends $a^{u_{2}}\!\!,\;b^{u_{2}}\!\!,\;c^{u_{2}}$ to $a^{bu_{2}}\!,\;b^{u_{2}}\!,\;c^{bu_{2}}$};\\[-2.0pt]
&\hskip 48.36967pt\text{$e$\; sends $a,b,c$ \;to\; $a,b^{c}\!,\;c$}\big\rangle,\end{split}

with “
d
sends
a^{u_{2}}
to
a^{bu_{2}}
”
simply meaning

a^{u_{2}d}=a^{bu_{2}}
.
For later purposes denote the set of generators of
\mathscr{A}
by

(5.11)

X_{\!\mathscr{A}}=\big\{a,b,c,t_{1},t^{\prime}_{1},u_{1},u_{2},d,e\big\}.

Denoting the set of defining relations of
\mathscr{A}
from (
5.10
) by

R_{\!\mathscr{A}}
we have

\mathscr{A}=\langle\,X_{\!\mathscr{A}}\;|\;R_{\!\mathscr{A}}\rangle
, i.e.,

\mathscr{A}
is given by
9
generators and
1+1+2+3+4+3+3+3=20
relations.

5.3.
Computing the conjugation of
a_{f}
by
d_{j}
in
\mathscr{A}

A useful computational feature takes place in
\mathscr{A}
.
Namely, using the earlier notation

f_{j}^{+}
​,

f_{j}^{-}
​,

f^{+}
​,

f^{-}

from Section
2.2

we can for a given
a_{f}
consider the elements, say,
a_{f_{j}^{+}}
or
a_{f^{-}}
in
\mathscr{A}
,
inside

F
.
Also, using the remark about
\langle d,e\rangle
in Section
2.4
we can use the elements
d_{i}
and
d_{f}
in
\mathscr{A}
,
outside

F
.

The following lemma uses this notation, and it is one of the main reasons for the sake of which the group
\mathscr{A}
was thus constructed:

Lemma 5.12
.

For any
f\in\mathcal{E}
and any
j\in{\mdmathbb Z}

we have

a_{f}^{d_{j}}=\!a_{f_{j}^{+}}

and

a_{f}^{\,d_{j}^{-1}}\!\!=a_{f_{j}^{-}}
in
\mathscr{A}
.

Its proof is uncomplicated after all preparations above. Hence we just bring simple examples that fully explain the argument.
Taking, say,
f=(2,5,3)
and
j=1
we write
b_{f}=b_{0}^{2}\,b_{1}^{5}\,b_{2}^{3}
and calculate the routine:

\begin{split}a_{f}^{d_{1}}&=\big(b_{2}^{\!-3}b_{1}^{\!-5}b_{0}^{\!-2}\;a\;b_{0}^{2}b_{1}^{5}b_{2}^{3}\,\big)^{d_{1}}\!\!\\
&=\big(b_{2}^{\!-3}\big)^{d_{1}}\big(b_{1}^{\!-5}\big)^{d_{1}}\big(b_{0}^{\!-2}\big)^{d_{1}}\;(a)^{d_{1}}\;\big(b_{0}^{2}\big)^{d_{1}}\big(b_{1}^{5}\big)^{d_{1}}\big(b_{2}^{3}\big)^{d_{1}}\!\!\\
&=b_{2}^{-3}\,\big(b_{1}^{\!-1}b_{1}^{\!-5}b_{1}^{\vphantom{8}}\big)\big(b_{1}^{\!-1}b_{0}^{\!-2}b_{1}^{\vphantom{8}}\big)\,\,\big(b_{1}^{\!-1}ab_{1}^{\vphantom{8}}\big)\,\big(b_{1}^{\!-1}b_{0}^{2}b_{1}^{\vphantom{8}}\big)\,\big(b_{1}^{\!-1}b_{1}^{5}b_{1}^{\vphantom{8}}\big)\,b_{2}^{3}\\
&=b_{2}^{\!-3}\big(b_{1}^{\!-1}b_{1}^{\!-5}\big)b_{0}^{\!-2}\;a\;b_{0}^{2}\big(b_{1}^{5}b_{1}^{1}\big)b_{2}^{3}\;=\;b_{2}^{\!-3}b_{1}^{\!-\,6}b_{0}^{\!-2}\;a\;b_{0}^{2}b_{1}^{6}b_{2}^{3}\\
&=a_{f_{1}^{+}}\end{split}

for the sequence

f_{1}^{+}\!=(2,\,\boldsymbol{5\!+\!1}\,,3)=(2,\boldsymbol{6},3)
.
Taking
j=2
we would have
a_{f}^{d_{2}}=a_{f_{2}^{+}}\!=a_{f^{+}}
where

f_{2}^{+}\!=f^{+}\!\!=(2,5,\,\boldsymbol{3\!+\!1})=(2,5,\boldsymbol{4})
.

Hopefully, the calculation routine in the displayed example does not entomb the simple meaning of Lemma
5.12
: the conjugation by
d^{j}
just “lifts” by
1
the exponent of the the factor corresponding to the
j
’th coordinate of
f
inside
a_{f}
.

Remark 5.13
.

The following feature of this lemma will be used repeatedly.
The
order
of elements
d_{i}
acting on
a_{f}
does
not
matter, i.e.,
a_{f}^{d_{j_{1}}d_{j_{2}}}
and
a_{f}^{d_{j_{2}}\,d_{j_{1}}}
are equal for any
f,j_{1},j_{2}
. Say, for the above
f=(2,5,3)
we have

a_{f}=a_{(2,5,3)}^{d_{1}d_{2}}=a_{(2,5,3)}^{d_{2}d_{1}}=a_{(2,\boldsymbol{6},\boldsymbol{4})}
.

6.
Theorem
A
and its proof steps

6.1.
Theorem
A
on benign subgroups

For notation of the sets
{\mathcal{E}}
,
{\mathcal{Z}}
,
{\mathcal{S}}
see Section
2.2
, and for the Higman operations
\iota,\upsilon,\rho,\sigma,\tau,\theta,\zeta,\pi,\omega_{m},

m=1,2,\ldots

in (
H
) over the subsets of
{\mathcal{E}}

see Section
2.3
.
Of these operations
\iota,\upsilon

are
binary
, and the rest are
unary
operations.
For the subgroup
A_{\!\mathcal{X}}
defined in the free group
F=\langle a,b,c\rangle
for a subset
\mathcal{X}\!\subseteq{\mathcal{E}}
see Section
2.4
.
If
A_{\!\mathcal{X}}
is benign in
F
, then we denote the respective finitely presented overgroup of
F
by
K_{\!\mathcal{X}}
, and the respective finitely generated subgroup of the latter by
L_{\!\mathcal{X}}
, see Section
2.5
.
Under an
explicitly given
group we understand a group explicitly given by its generators and defining relations, see Section
2.1
.

Figure 4.
An illustration for statement (
3
) in Theorem
A
.

With this notation the theorem below allows to explicitly build new benign subgroups from the existing ones:

Theorem A
.

Let
\mathcal{Y}
be a subset of
{\mathcal{E}}
for one of the following cases:

1.

\mathcal{Y}
is one of sets and
{\mathcal{Z}}
or
{\mathcal{S}}
;

2.

\mathcal{Y}
is obtained from sets
\mathcal{X}_{1},\mathcal{X}_{2}\subseteq{\mathcal{E}}
by any of operations

\iota
or
\upsilon
,
with
A_{\mathcal{X}_{1}}
,
A_{\mathcal{X}_{2}}
benign in
F
;

3.

\mathcal{Y}
is obtained from
\mathcal{X}\subseteq{\mathcal{E}}
by any of operations

\rho,\sigma,\tau,\theta,\zeta,\pi,\omega_{m}
, with
A_{\mathcal{X}}
benign in
F
.

Then
A_{\mathcal{Y}}
is benign in
F
. Moreover, for case (
1
) the groups
K_{\mathcal{Y}}
and
L_{\mathcal{Y}}
can always be given explicitly.
For case (
2
) they can be given explicitly, if
K_{\mathcal{X}_{1}}
,
K_{\mathcal{X}_{2}}
and

L_{\mathcal{X}_{1}}
,
L_{\mathcal{X}_{2}}
are known explicitly.
For case (
3
)
they can be given explicitly, if
K_{\mathcal{X}}
and
L_{\mathcal{X}}
are known explicitly.

The proof will be given in sections
6.2
–
6.11
below, and the promised groups
K_{\mathcal{Y}}
and
L_{\mathcal{Y}}
will be written down explicitly in
6.2
,

6.3
,

6.5.3
,

6.6.3
,

6.7.2
,

6.8.2
,

6.9.5
,

6.10.4
,

6.11.6
.

Remark 6.1
.

Comparing this theorem with lemmas 4.4 – 4.10 in
[
15
]
we see that the only novelty of Theorem
A
is that it constructs an
explicit
finitely presented group
K_{\mathcal{Y}}
and its
explicit
finitely generated subgroup
L_{\mathcal{Y}}
for each
\mathcal{Y}
involved. Also, Theorem
A
makes sure each
A_{\mathcal{Y}}
is benign in the
same
free group
F
of rank
3

necessarily
, see differences stressed in Section
4.4
.
These features are required for explicit embedding of recursive groups later.
We stress that some of the steps of Himgan’s construction
already are explicit
in
[
15
]
, and for them we do not need the constructions developed in
[
32
]
.

The extra “auxiliary” Higman operations

\sigma^{i},\zeta_{i},\zeta_{S},\pi^{\prime},\pi_{i},\pi^{\prime}_{i},\tau_{k,l},\alpha,\epsilon_{S},+,\;\iota_{n},\upsilon_{n}
of (
H*
) were introduced in
[
31
]
to simplify usage of the Higman operations, see Section
2.3
above. As we have seen in
Section 2.4 of
[
31
]
, each of (
H*
) is a combination of some of original Higman operations (
H
). Hence the analog of Theorem
A
holds true for extra “auxiliary” operations (
H*
) also.

6.2.
The proof for the case of
{\mathcal{Z}}
and
{\mathcal{S}}

The first case
\mathcal{Y}={\mathcal{Z}}
is trivial by Remark
2.1
:

A_{{\mathcal{Z}}}
is benign in
F
simply because
A_{{\mathcal{Z}}}=\langle a_{(0)}\rangle=\langle a\rangle
,
defined by a
single
function
f\!=\!(0)
, is a finitely generated (cyclic) group, and we can just pick

K_{{\mathcal{Z}}}=F
and
L_{{\mathcal{Z}}}=\langle a\rangle
.

For the second case
\mathcal{Y}={\mathcal{S}}
as the finitely presented group
K_{{\mathcal{S}}}
choose
\mathscr{A}
from Section
5.2
.
Since
{\mathcal{S}}
contains the function
f\!=\!(0,1)
, then repeatedly using the technical Lemma
5.12
with
a_{f}=a_{(0,1)}
for
2n
times we have:

(6.1)

a_{(0,1)}^{(d_{0}d_{1})^{n}}\!\!=\big(a_{(0,1)}^{d_{0}}\big)^{d_{1}\,(d_{0}d_{1})^{n-1}}\!\!\!\!=a_{(0+1,\,1)}^{d_{1}\,(d_{0}d_{1})^{n-1}}\!\!\!=a_{(1,\,1+1)}^{(d_{0}d_{1})^{n-1}}\!\!\!=a_{(2,\,2+1)}^{(d_{0}d_{1})^{n-2}}\!\!\!=\cdots=a_{(n,n+1)}\,,

that is,
a_{(n,n+1)}
belongs to
\langle a_{f},\;d_{0}d_{1}\rangle
for any
n\in{\mdmathbb Z}
.
Since we also have
a_{(n,n+1)}\in F
, then
A_{{\mathcal{S}}}\subseteq F\cap\langle a_{(0,1)},\;d_{0}d_{1}\rangle
holds.

On the other hand, applying the “conjugates collecting” process (
2.4
)
for
x=a_{(0,1)}

and

y=d_{0}d_{1}
,
we can rewrite
any
element
w\in\langle a_{(0,1)},\;d_{0}d_{1}\rangle

as
w=u\cdot v
, where
u
is a product of some conjugates
x^{\pm y^{n_{i}}}\!=a_{(0,1)}^{\pm(d_{0}d_{1})^{n_{i}}}
​​​​,  and
v
is equal to
y^{k}=(d_{0}d_{1})^{k}
for a certain
n_{i},k
.
By (
6.1
) all those conjugates are in
F
.
Thus, if we additionally show that from
w\in F
it follows
v\in F
, then we will have

F\cap\langle a_{f},\;d_{0}d_{1}\rangle\subseteq A_{{\mathcal{S}}}
, which together with the previous inclusion means
F\cap\langle a_{f},\;d_{0}d_{1}\rangle=A_{{\mathcal{S}}}
, i.e.,
A_{{\mathcal{S}}}
is benign for the above
K_{{\mathcal{S}}}
and its finitely generated subgroup

L_{{\mathcal{S}}}=\langle a_{f},\;d_{0}d_{1}\rangle
.
As an explicit presentation of
K_{{\mathcal{S}}}=\mathscr{A}
just pick (
5.10
).

It remains to verify that
v\in F
takes place for
v=1
only.

\mathscr{A}
is the “nested” HNN-extension
